(numbers and the csv or file they come from), and the commit that carries it. The final report artifact
is generated from this file plus the csvs under `.agents/tmp/token-diet/`, never from memory.

`docs/audits/2026-09-02-cheap-model-delegation-report.md`, section "Token diet".

## 2026-09-08 — cf-lfg autonomy contract, simplify step, recommendation-applying

Changed `clis/cheap-flow/skills/{cf-lfg,cf-brainstorm,cf-review}/SKILL.md` after
an orchestrated `/cf-lfg` run handed back to the user after step 1 instead of
continuing. Four defects, all textual:

1. `cf-brainstorm` step 6 said "Stop. Say what the next step is (`/cf-plan`)"
   **unconditionally**, and `cf-review` step 3 ended "Next step: `/cf-compound`"
   the same way. Under `/cf-lfg` those lines instruct the exact hand-back the
   orchestrator forbids. Both are now conditional on how the skill was invoked.
2. `cf-lfg` never stated that the run may not return to the user before its
   final report, nor that a sub-skill's closing line is suppressed. Added.
3. `cf-lfg` step 2 gated only on `check <slug>` printing `ok`, so the plan-check
   researcher's recommendations could be left unapplied. Now each must be
   applied or explicitly dropped in `contract.md`.
4. No simplify pass. The reference plugin
   (github.com/everyinc/compound-engineering-plugin) runs `/ce-simplify-code`
   before review; `/lfg` there is "plans, works through the plan, simplifies,
   runs code review and applies the fixes". Added as step 4, restructured to the
   findings-to-units pattern so Claude still never edits code itself.

**Benchmark decision: no eval run, deliberately.** The rule is the smallest spend
that still answers the question. The question here is "does an orchestrated run
still stop after a step", and the cause was three quotable instruction lines that
told it to; the fix is that those lines are now conditional. A `skill-eval` of
`/cf-lfg` costs a full pipeline (brainstorm + plan + n work units + review +
compound) per sample to observe one control-flow property that is now settled by
reading the file. Prior evidence rules the run out. The behavioural check instead
rides the next real `/cf-lfg` invocation: if it reaches step 8 and emits
`<promise>DONE</promise>` without an intervening user turn, the contract holds.
Re-open with a real eval if a future run hands back anyway.

## 2026-10-06: wf Rust migration, baseline (phase 1)

`wf bench` (clis/workflow-cli) measures each command in a fresh process:
wall time and peak RSS from python3 `resource.getrusage(RUSAGE_CHILDREN)`, plus
bundle sizes and the `node_modules` entry count. `WF_BIN` selects the binary, so
the same command benchmarks the Rust `wf` later. Neither `/usr/bin/time` nor
`hyperfine` exists in the container.

Baseline: TypeScript `wf` bundle, node v22.22.0, x86_64, 4 cores, n=5, startup
class commands only (no network or VM; `--live` adds `dispatch --stats` and
`deploy plan`).

| target | median wall ms | median RSS MB | max RSS MB |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `--version` | 266.8 | 64.2 | 64.4 |
| `--help` | 266.0 | 64.5 | 64.5 |
| `secrets --help` | 268.9 | 64.3 | 64.4 |
| `dispatch --help` | 266.2 | 64.2 | 64.2 |
| `deploy plan --help` | 315.8 | 64.3 | 64.4 |
| `verify --help` | 266.3 | 64.5 | 64.5 |

Footprint: `wf.js` 1.94 MB, caveat-guard bundle 0.77 MB, agent-config-sync
bundle 0.58 MB; `node_modules` holds 4,406,964 entries (recursive listing of the
dev tree, not the production image).

Reading: every command, even `--version`, costs about 266 ms and 64 MB, so the
price is Node startup plus loading the whole bundle, not the command. Wall times
cluster at about 266 and 316 ms, which suggests coarse timer or scheduler steps
in this container; treat differences under 50 ms as noise.

Decision: keep this script as the before/after harness for every ported group.
Live-command rows (`verify --staged`, `dispatch --stats`, `secrets sync-env`,
`deploy plan`) are deferred to a run with credentials and VM access.

## 2026-10-06: wf Rust migration, dependency memory audit (phase 2)

`wf bench --deps` imports each startup dependency alone in a fresh node process
(`import()` from the package that owns it) and subtracts an empty `node -e 0`.
Node v22.22.0, x86_64, n=5, peak RSS from python3 rusage.

| dep | median RSS MB | delta MB |
| --- | --- | --- |
| (empty node process) | 41.7 | 0 |
| commander | 52.3 | 10.6 |
| yaml | 52.9 | 11.1 |
| pngjs | 52.1 | 10.4 |
| @opentelemetry/api | 51.6 | 9.9 |
| loglayer | 50.0 | 8.3 |
| pixelmatch | 42.8 | 1.0 |
| playwright | 137.1 | 95.4 |

Wall time in this container advances in 50 ms steps (every sample is a multiple
of about 50 ms), so the millisecond columns are not usable here; playwright is
the only delta that clears that resolution (about 755 ms over empty). RSS is
the figure to trust. The deltas are not additive: the first fs, stream or
buffer-using import pays the shared node internals, which explains four deps at
about 10 MB.

Reading:
- The Node floor is 41.7 MB before any wf code; the whole `wf --help` costs
  64 MB, so about two thirds of the footprint is runtime, not dependencies.
  A Rust binary removes the floor, which no dependency swap in TS can.
- Playwright (95 MB, about 755 ms) is not imported by `wf --help`, so it only
  matters on browser commands. That makes multi-browser the last port and a
  candidate for a separate process (CDP from Rust, or a Node sidecar spawned
  only for browser commands) rather than something the dispatcher loads.
- commander, yaml, pngjs, @opentelemetry/api and loglayer map to `clap`,
  `serde_yaml`, `image` (PNG only), `tracing` and `tracing`; none of them is
  worth replacing in TypeScript first.

Decision: do not swap dependencies inside the TS CLIs; go straight to the Rust
skeleton. Re-run `wf bench --deps` only for any dependency that survives in the
fallback bundle.

## 2026-10-06: wf Rust migration, black-box test shape (phase 3)

The black-box harness already exists: `tests/helpers/run-wf.ts` spawns the
bundle with stub executables prepended to `PATH` (`createStub`, `setupWfTest`),
and the suites under `tests/integration/cli` assert stdout, exit code and the
argv the stubs logged. `runWf` now honours `WF_BIN`: when set it runs that
executable directly, so the same suite can target the Rust binary unchanged.
Verified with `tests/integration/cli/wf-bin-override.integration.test.ts` and the
existing 34 integration tests, all against the TypeScript bundle.

Decision: unit tests are not deleted in this step. Deleting them before a Rust
command exists would drop coverage with nothing to replace it. Each port PR
deletes the unit tests of the command group it moves, in the same diff, once the
black-box suite covers that group against `WF_BIN`. A group without black-box
coverage gets it added in TypeScript first, in that PR, before the port.

## 2026-10-06: wf Rust migration, skeleton (phase 4)

`clis/Cargo.toml` is the workspace, `clis/wf` the binary. It dispatches on the
first argument: `--version` is native, a `NATIVE` table holds ported commands
(empty), and everything else, plus the hidden `__legacy <argv>`, `exec`s
`node <bundle>` so the fallback adds no second process. `WF_LEGACY_BUNDLE`
overrides the bundle path; `WF_NODE` the node binary (the black-box harness
sets it so a stubbed `node` on PATH cannot capture the fallback). Argument
parsing with `clap` arrives with the first native command, not before: with no
native command there is nothing for it to parse.

Verification (tiers): Rust integration tests `clis/wf/tests/dispatch.rs`
(4 tests, 25 of 25 stress runs green after serializing the fake-node spawns,
which otherwise hit ETXTBSY); the existing black-box suite
`tests/integration/cli` (34 tests) green against both the TypeScript bundle and
the Rust binary through `WF_BIN`. No deployed-service tier applies yet.

Benchmark (release build, opt-level z, lto, strip, n=5, same container):

| target | TypeScript wf | Rust wf |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `--version` wall ms | 266.8 | 3.9 |
| `--version` peak RSS MB | 64.2 | 10.9 |
| `--help` (legacy fallback) wall ms | 266.0 | 265.7 |
| `--help` (legacy fallback) peak RSS MB | 64.5 | 64.7 |
| binary size | 1.94 MB bundle plus node | 316 KB |

Reading: a native command costs a few milliseconds and 11 MB, and the fallback
costs the same as before plus nothing measurable, so the win accrues exactly per
ported command, as planned.

Open decision, not yet taken: distribution. Hooks, lefthook and CI call
`node clis/workflow-cli/bundle/wf.js` directly, and a Rust binary cannot be
committed like a bundle (per-arch: x86_64 here, aarch64 on the VM). `bin/wf`
therefore still points at the TypeScript bundle in this PR. The proposal is to
build the binary in `.agents/setup.sh` and the `gh-runner` image, repoint the
hook call sites to `bin/wf`, then flip `bin/wf` to the Rust binary in the next
PR. That touches CI and the VM image, so it is called out for review rather than
done silently.

## 2026-10-06: wf Rust migration, port order revised (phase 5 entry)

Workspace dependencies per CLI (`package.json` `dependencies`):

| CLI | non-test TS lines | workspace and third-party deps |
| --- | --- | --- |
| agent-config-sync | 2.2k | none (imports `libraries/hook-runtime` by relative path) |
| secrets | 3.6k | `@lib/logging`, commander, dotenv, zod |
| vnc-auth-capture | 3.8k | `@cli/multi-browser`, `@cli/secrets`, `@lib/logging`, commander, zod |
| caveat-guard | 3.3k | `@app/remote-chat-agent` (the 25k-line runner) |
| multi-browser | 11.8k | Playwright |
| workflow-cli | 25.4k | all of the above plus `@app/remote-chat-agent`, `@lib/*` |

The plan listed caveat-guard first by size. It is the wrong first port: it
judges every turn by calling `@app/remote-chat-agent/run` (opencode), is
installed as a marketplace plugin from a committed bundle, and would need that
runner reimplemented or put behind a process boundary. New order:
agent-config-sync (no deps; output is byte-comparable, so the TypeScript run is
the oracle: sync both on the repo and diff), then secrets, then wf command groups
that do not touch the runner, then vnc-auth-capture, caveat-guard and
multi-browser, with the runner seam decided before caveat-guard.

Gate before any hook-called CLI is ported: binary distribution. agent-config-sync
is called as the `mcp-guard` PreToolUse hook on every MCP call and as
`sync:check`; hooks, lefthook and CI call committed bundles with
`node clis/*/bundle/*.js`. Replacing one with a Rust binary that is absent on a
fresh container or runner would silently disable the guard. The binary must be
built in `.agents/setup.sh` and in the `gh-runner` image (x86_64 and aarch64)
and the call sites repointed before the first hook-called port lands.

## 2026-10-06: wf Rust migration, binary distribution decision

Decision (taken, not deferred): the Rust `wf` is built where it is used.
`.agents/setup.sh` gains `step_wf_rust`: `cargo build --release -p wf` (a no-op
when unchanged) and a `~/.local/bin/wf-rs` link to `clis/target/release/wf`.
`wf-rs` is a separate name until the first port lands, so nothing that calls
`wf` changes. Rules for the ports that follow:

- A CLI that runs as a hook (`mcp-guard`, the Stop judge) must fail closed when
  the binary is missing: block with exit 2 and say so. A missing guard may never
  read as an allow.
- Without cargo (a bare container) setup warns and the TypeScript bundle stays
  the only `wf`; no port is merged until the `gh-runner` image and the VM build
  carry cargo too. aarch64 is unverified here (the container is x86_64).

Verified: `step_wf_rust` run in isolation built the binary and linked it;
`wf-rs --version` prints 1.0.0.

## 2026-10-06: agent-config-sync in Rust (first port, beside the TypeScript)

`clis/agent-config-sync/src-rs` is a complete Rust implementation of the
TypeScript CLI: `sync`, `sync --check` and the `mcp-guard` PreToolUse hook, with
the same contract validation (zod strictness and key order reproduced), the
same renderers for Claude, Codex and opencode, and the same exit codes
(`mcp-guard` failures exit 1 and say `NOT ENFORCING`, an unwritable checkout
skips with exit 0). It is a Cargo workspace member next to `wf`.

Parity evidence (tiers: black-box integration against the real binary, then the
real repo):
- The real repo: `agent-config-sync --check` exits 0 with zero drift against the
  files the TypeScript wrote, so every generated file (all AGENTS.md and
  CLAUDE.md areas, `.claude/settings.json`, `.mcp.json`, `.codex/*`,
  `opencode.json`, copied skills) is byte-identical.
- The fixture of the TypeScript test helpers: `tests-rs/golden` is what the
  TypeScript `sync` wrote for it, and `parity.rs` asserts the Rust output equals
  it byte for byte (23 files, executable bit on `.claude/setup.sh`). The deny
  decision of `mcp-guard` is compared byte for byte against the TypeScript
  output too.
- 9 Rust integration tests, Given/When/Then: golden parity, idempotent second
  sync, drift naming after a source edit, stale and legacy removal, symlink
  replacement, Claude-written settings keys surviving in place, an invalid
  command failing loud, the guard's tiers, a broken `mcps.json` failing loud.

Benchmark (release build, same container, n=5, python3 rusage; the guard row
includes the `sh -c` wrapper that feeds stdin):

| command | TypeScript | Rust |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `sync --check` wall / peak RSS | 270.3 ms / 68.0 MB | 30.0 ms / 11.0 MB |
| `mcp-guard` wall / peak RSS | 162.6 ms / 57.4 MB | 10.5 ms / 10.9 MB |
| binary | 0.58 MB bundle plus node | 1.88 MB |

The guard runs on every MCP tool call, so its 150 ms and 46 MB are paid per
call today. The binary is larger than the bundle because `regex` and `toml`
bring Unicode tables; the bundle needs a 40 MB runtime on top of it.

Known differences, all on failure paths or edge inputs, none on valid configs:
zod's error text is not reproduced (the Rust messages name the same file and
reason), `.` in the tool-name regex stops at `\n` only where JS also stops at
`\r`, and a whole-valued float such as `60.0` is normalised to `60` as JS
prints it.

Not done in this PR, deliberately: the TypeScript is not deleted and no call
site is repointed. `package.json`, `.agents/setup.sh`, the e2e tests and the
`wf agents mcp-guard` dispatch still call the bundle. Repointing the PreToolUse
guard must wait for the gate recorded above (cargo in the `gh-runner` image and
the VM, aarch64 verified), because a missing binary must block, not allow.

## 2026-10-06: wf secrets read path in Rust (second port, native in the Rust wf)

`@cli/secrets` is not a standalone binary: `wf` mounts its commander command, and
backend services import the same package as a library (`load`,
`getLoadedSecretValues`). So the Rust port lives in the Rust `wf`
(`clis/wf/src/secrets`), and the TypeScript package stays until the last Node
consumer moves. The dispatcher hands any input the Rust side does not recognise
(help, unknown options, `set`, `delete`, `push-notes`, `create`, `rename`,
`update`) to the TypeScript CLI, so behaviour there is unchanged.

Ported natively: the `list`, `get`, `env`, `sync-env` (including `--for`,
`--base`, `--out`, `--reveal`, the redacted default preview, placeholder and
ambient-environment handling) and `doctor` (`--if-available`, drift report)
subcommands. Decisions taken without asking, per the brief:
- **Bitwarden only for the vault, via `bws`.** The macOS Keychain lookup is
  ported (read-only, `security find-generic-password`) because bootstrap tokens
  use it, but it is untested here: the container is Linux. Keychain writes stay
  TypeScript.
- **Telemetry through `curl`.** The Rust binary posts the same ingest contract
  with `curl --config -`, token and body on stdin, never argv. The TypeScript
  scrubbing layer is not ported: every field is a backend name, a registry key
  name, a count or a boolean by construction, and a test asserts a secret value
  is never in the payload. A failed post still fails the command, as before.
- **dotenv parsing** reimplements `dotenv.parse`'s line grammar (quotes,
  `export`, inline comments, `\n` in double quotes); the black-box suite pins the
  shapes the repo uses (`.env.defaults` merge). Multi-line quoted values are not
  covered.
- **The registry is embedded at build time** from the one
  `clis/secrets/src/data/registry.json`, so there is no second copy. The
  `APP_KEY_GROUPS` table is a Rust copy, and a test fails if it drifts: it
  asserts, for every group, the TypeScript export against what the binary emits.

Verification (tier: black-box integration against the real binary with a stub
`bws`, no real vault): `tests/integration/cli/secrets.integration.test.ts`, 32
Given/When/Then tests, runs unchanged against the TypeScript bundle and the Rust
binary through `WF_BIN`; the TypeScript run was the oracle and corrected five of
my own expectations (bootstrap token listed by the env command, 8-dot mask,
`missing` padding). Both pass 66 of 66 with the existing integration tests.
Rust-only tests (`clis/wf/tests/secrets_telemetry.rs`, 4) cover the `curl`
transport, status failure, the kill switch and the unset-source case.

Benchmark (release, same container, n=5, every registry variable exported so no
vault call is made, so this is the pure CLI cost):

| command | TypeScript wf | Rust wf |
| --- | --- | --- |
| sync-env `--for reporting-service --reveal` | 261.2 ms / 65.2 MB | 8.0 ms / 11.0 MB |
| list | 256.1 ms / 64.8 MB | 5.3 ms / 11.0 MB |
| binary | 1.94 MB bundle plus node | 1.36 MB |

Not verified: a real vault (stubbed `bws` only), macOS, aarch64.

## 2026-10-06: wf bench in Rust (third port), and a correction to the baseline

`wf bench` is now native in the Rust `wf` (`clis/wf/src/bench.rs`). It no longer
needs python3: peak RSS comes from `wait4`, which returns the rusage of exactly
the child it reaped, so each run is still a fresh process measured on its own.
`--deps` (the Node import audit) is left to the TypeScript CLI, since it prices
Node itself. Same flags otherwise (`--runs`, `--live`, `--out`), plus a new
`--no-footprint` in both implementations. The report drops the `(node vX)`
suffix and the `node` JSON field, because the subject of the benchmark is
whatever `WF_BIN` names, not the runner.

**Correction to the phase 1 baseline.** The TypeScript footprint counted
`node_modules` with a recursive `readdirSync`, which follows pnpm's symlinks and
counts each package once per link. The baseline logged 4,406,964 entries and the
count took 82 s. The real number is 256,087 entries. Both implementations now
walk without following symlinks and agree (256,087 on this tree), the
TypeScript count takes 1.2 s and the Rust one 0.49 s. The baseline's other
numbers (startup, RSS, bundle sizes) are unaffected. Any figure derived from the
old count (the "37k files in the production tree" in the greenfield ledger is a
different, production-image measurement) should be read against 256,087.

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `tests/integration/cli/bench.integration.test.ts`
(5 tests, Given/When/Then, a fake shell-script binary as the subject) passes
against both the TypeScript bundle and the Rust binary through `WF_BIN`. The
footprint half is covered for Rust by `clis/wf/tests/bench_footprint.rs` (a
temporary tree with a bundle and a `node_modules`); the black-box suite passes
`--no-footprint` because walking a real install is slow under any binary.

## 2026-10-06: wf agents and wf caveat in Rust (fourth port)

`wf agents ...` and `wf caveat ...` are the two passthrough commands. In the
TypeScript `wf` each one spawns a second node process on a sibling CLI's bundle.
In the Rust `wf`:
- `agents` runs the agent-config-sync library crate **in the same process**
  (`agent_config_sync::cli::run`, the function the standalone binary also uses),
  so `wf agents mcp-guard`, the command the PreToolUse hook dispatcher actually
  runs on every MCP call, is one small process instead of two node processes.
  Failure output is unchanged: the child's stderr, then `wf: <tail>`.
- `caveat on|off|status|--help` is the local flag-file toggle, natively. Any other
  argument returns "not mine" and the dispatcher forwards the original argv to
  the TypeScript `wf`, which forwards to caveat-guard (still TypeScript, blocked
  on the 25k-line runner it imports).

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `tests/integration/cli/agents-caveat.integration.test.ts`
(9 Given/When/Then tests: usage, the on/off flag file in a temporary git repo,
and the guard's tiers plus its byte-exact deny and loud failure) passes against
both the TypeScript bundle and the Rust binary through `WF_BIN`. The whole
integration directory passes 80 of 80 on the Rust binary. The `agents` cases run
inside the repository because a passthrough resolves its sibling CLI from the
repo root; they feed tool names to the guard and never call an MCP tool.

Benchmark (release, same container, n=5, `sh -c` wrapper included in the RSS):

| command | TypeScript wf | Rust wf |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `agents mcp-guard` (the per-MCP-call hook path) | 409.3 ms / 64.3 MB | 11.6 ms / 10.9 MB |
| binary | 1.94 MB bundle plus node | 2.1 MB (all ported commands) |

The hook path was 160 ms in the standalone-binary comparison; going through the
TypeScript `wf` as the dispatcher really does made it 409 ms, so the saving per
MCP call is about 400 ms and 53 MB.

## 2026-10-06: the Rust wf now loads the env files the TypeScript wf loads (a fix to #316)

Found while reading the TypeScript entrypoint for the next port: before any
command, `wf` loads `~/.config/wf/.env`, `<repo>/.env` and `<repo>/.env.local`
into `process.env` (`loadWfEnv`), with the shell always winning and repo files
read only when a repo root exists. The Rust `wf` did not, so for `wf secrets`
a value in `.env.local` was ignored: `get` and `sync-env` answered from the vault
where the TypeScript answered from the file. That is a behavioural difference in
a credential path, in my own PR #316, and the black-box suite did not see it
because it ran with a clean environment in a directory with no repo.

Fix: `clis/wf/src/env_file.rs` reimplements `loadWfEnv` (precedence, `$VAR`
expansion with `$HOME` special-cased, one layer of unquoting with escapes only in
double quotes, `REPO_ROOT` discovery) and runs before a native command. If the
command then hands off to the TypeScript `wf`, the variables it set are removed
first, so the TypeScript loads its own and sees the same shell it always did.

Evidence (tier: black-box integration): six new `wf env files` tests in
`secrets.integration.test.ts` (`.env.local` shadows the vault and is reported as
ambient, `.env.local` over `.env`, shell over files, user file under repo files,
expansion and unquoting, a stray `.env` outside a repository ignored). They were
written first against the TypeScript bundle, which passed all six; the Rust
binary failed four of them before the fix and passes all six after. The whole
integration directory (24 files, 152 tests) passes on the Rust binary.

Lesson recorded for the remaining ports: read the top-level entrypoint of the
TypeScript CLI (`cli.ts`), not just the command being ported, because wiring
that runs before every command (env files, logging and telemetry setup, error
formatting) is part of its behaviour.

## 2026-10-06: the signed vm-ops-api client in Rust (fifth port)

Native in the Rust `wf`: `wf vm-ops health|logs|redeploy-self|push-bootstrap-token`
and `wf vm ledger cat`. This is the client every VM-facing command builds on
(`deploy`, `vm exec`, `vm recover`, the dispatch live tee), so it is ported as a
module (`vmops.rs`) with the commands as thin handlers (`vm_ops.rs`).

What is reimplemented: the request signing of `@lib/vm-request-auth` (rotating
token `HMAC-SHA256(seed, UTC date)` plus an Ed25519 signature over
`METHOD\npath\ntimestamp\nsha256(body)`), PEM handling including the vault's
one-line `\n` form, the `curl` invocation through tailscaled's SOCKS5 listener
(`WF_TAILNET_SOCKS`, empty means direct), status parsing, the full-output error
body for failed redeploys, `encodeURIComponent`, and credential resolution
through the secrets loader (`load_values`, the Rust counterpart of the library's
`load([...keys])`). New dependencies: `ed25519-dalek`, `hmac`, `sha2`, `base64`.
`--json`, help and every other `vm` subcommand (`exec`, `logs`, `recover`) still go
to the TypeScript.

Verification (tier: black-box integration, real signature verification): 15
Given/When/Then tests in `vm-ops.integration.test.ts`. A stub `curl` records its
argv and stdin and answers `body\nstatus`; the signature and rotating token it
received are checked with the real `verifySignature` and `deriveRotatingToken`
from `@lib/vm-request-auth` against a freshly generated Ed25519 key, so a wrong
canonical string or key handling fails the test. They pass against the TypeScript
bundle (written first, passed first time) and against the Rust binary (also first
time). Together with the earlier suites, the integration directory is 167 of 167
on the Rust binary.

Benchmark (release, same container, n=5, stub `curl`, so this is the client
cost, not the network): `vm-ops health` 313.4 ms / 65.7 MB in the TypeScript
`wf`, 24.3 ms / 10.9 MB in Rust. The Rust binary is 2.26 MB with the crypto
crates (2.1 MB before).

Not verified: a real vm-ops-api over a real tailnet (no tailnet here); the
signature scheme is verified against the TypeScript verifier, not the server's
mirrored copy in `infra/oracle-cloud/vm-ops-api/src/http/auth.ts`.

## 2026-10-06: `wf vm exec` in Rust (sixth port)

`wf vm exec` runs a local script inside agent-shell through vm-ops-api's
start/poll/cancel job model, on top of the signed client from the previous port.
Native: `--status <id>`, `--cancel <id>`, `<file>` with `--no-wait` (prints the
job id), the default streaming mode (only newly appeared output is printed, the
job id goes to stderr, the job's exit code is the command's), and `--out <dir>`
with repeatable `--artifact <rel>` (64 KiB script cap, 25-minute deadline with a
cancel, six consecutive poll failures tolerated, every artifact fetched whatever
the exit code, `..` in an artifact path refused before any request). `--e2e`,
`--branch`, `--json` and help still go to the TypeScript.

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `vm-exec.integration.test.ts`, 11
Given/When/Then tests. A stub `curl` answers each call from a numbered response
file and records that call's argv and stdin, so a whole start, poll, fetch
sequence is scripted and the URL, method and body of every call are asserted
(including `URLSearchParams` encoding of artifact paths). Written first against
the TypeScript bundle (passed first time), then run against the Rust binary
(passed first time). The integration directory is 178 of 178 on the Rust binary.

Known difference: the TypeScript logs a logfmt warning to stderr on each failed
poll in `--out` mode; the Rust version retries silently. The error message when
the script file does not exist also differs in wording (both exit 1).

Not verified: a real job on a real agent-shell. The polling interval is the
TypeScript's fixed 2 s, so the one streaming test spends two seconds sleeping.

## 2026-10-06: `wf vm logs` in Rust (seventh port)

`wf vm logs [app]` is a one-shot BetterStack read through the ClickHouse HTTP
interface: basic auth, the SQL as the raw `plain/text` body, always
`remote(<table>)`. Native in the Rust `wf` (`vm_logs.rs`): the four
`CH_<SOURCE>_*` settings (filled from the vault first, with the placeholder and
missing-setting messages unchanged), `--since` as an explicit ClickHouse
INTERVAL (a bare `m` would silently widen the window), the level, module, grep
and app filters with the TypeScript's escaping, the detection of a ClickHouse
error that curl reported as success, and the line format including OTLP
attributes. `--source`, `--limit` and the other options match; `--json` and help
go to the TypeScript. A failing `curl` exits with its own code, as before.

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `vm-logs.integration.test.ts`, 14
Given/When/Then tests asserting the exact argv `curl` receives (including the SQL
for no filters, every filter together with quote and backslash escaping, the
three interval units and the mobile source), the formatted lines for a JSON row,
an OTLP row and a non-JSON row, and every failure path. Written first against the
TypeScript bundle (passed first time), then the Rust binary (passed first time).

Observation, not changed here: the ClickHouse password travels in `curl`'s argv
(`-u user:password`), as it does in the TypeScript, so it is visible in the
process list while the query runs. The Rust version keeps argv parity so the
black-box suite stays language-neutral. Moving the credential to `curl --config -`
on stdin, as the telemetry client already does, is a small change for both
implementations once the TypeScript is gone.

Not verified: a real BetterStack/ClickHouse endpoint. `report-health`, which
shares `betterstack.ts`, is not ported.

## 2026-10-06: `wf deploy status|plan|metro|retire` in Rust (eighth port)

The deployer's read commands and the planning step CI runs on every push to
`dev`, native in the Rust `wf` (`deploy.rs`) on top of the signed vm-ops client:
- `status [apps...]`: container status per app (a failing app becomes an
  `{error}` entry, not a failure), then `/health` and `/redeploy/status`, in the
  TypeScript's call order.
- `plan [--force] [--github-output]`: which of the three legs (`remote-chat-agent`,
  `leetcode-backend`, `leetcode-auth-cookie-capture`) and whether Metro a push
  must deploy: the commit each running image was built from (its `sha-<short>`
  tag), `git cat-file` to know the commit, `pnpm --filter ...[sha] list` for the
  affected packages (once per distinct commit), `git diff --quiet` for the paths
  copied into an image outside the workspace graph. An unreachable service or an
  unknown commit plans as changed. `--github-output` appends `legs=` and `metro=`
  to `$GITHUB_OUTPUT`.
- `metro [--branch] [--commit] [--logs]`: the shared dev Metro through
  dev-bridge: start (15 minute limit), status parse (`alive`, `commit`, `url`),
  and the failure when it does not answer.
- `retire <app>`.
Left to the TypeScript: `deploy run` (builds images on the VM through a generated
script, with signal handling and transport retries), `sync-creds`, `tf`,
`agent-deps`, `--json` and help.

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `deploy.integration.test.ts`, 14
Given/When/Then tests with stub `curl` (numbered replies, argv recorded), `git`
and `pnpm`, so whole plans are scripted: nothing changed, a package changed,
extra paths changed, an unreachable service, a dead Metro, and a commit the
checkout does not know. Written first against the TypeScript bundle (passed first
time), then the Rust binary (passed first time). The integration directory is 206
of 206 on the Rust binary.

Benchmark (release, same container, n=5): `deploy plan --force` (no network)
255.4 ms / 64.5 MB in the TypeScript `wf`, 5.8 ms / 11.0 MB in Rust. The
unforced plan is dominated by the `pnpm` calls it makes (up to one per distinct
deployed commit), which no `wf` rewrite changes.

Not verified: a real deployer or a real workspace graph.

## 2026-10-06: `wf deploy run` in Rust (ninth port), and a bug it exposed

`wf deploy run` builds images natively on the arm64 VM (inside agent-shell's
docker-in-docker) and pushes them, redeploys an app at a tag through vm-ops-api,
or does both in sequence. Native in the Rust `wf`: the generator of the `sh`
script (`build_image.rs`), the build job lifecycle with deploy's settings (10 s
poll, 18 failed polls tolerated, only new output streamed), the redeploy with its
transport-only retry (12 attempts, 15 s apart, never on an HTTP answer), the
cache-export job started after a redeploy, every validation message, and the
stop handling: the first SIGINT or SIGTERM cancels the VM job and exits 143, a
second leaves at once with 128 plus the signal. `--json`, a missing `--ref`
(commander's own error) and help still go to the TypeScript.

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `deploy-run.integration.test.ts`, 22
Given/When/Then tests. A stub `curl` plays vm-ops-api (numbered replies, the last
one repeats, per-call exit codes, argv and stdin recorded). The `sh` scripts sent
to the VM are compared **byte for byte** with golden files written by the
TypeScript generator (five scenarios: single image with a tag, three images with
sidecars, no tag, the full release of `remote-chat-agent` with its companions,
and the cache export), so a changed character in a build script fails the test.
One test starts the real process, waits for the job to be polled, sends SIGTERM
and asserts exit 143 and a POST to the job's cancel route. The goldens were
generated from the TypeScript bundle first; the Rust binary matched them and
passed all 22 first time.

**A bug in the TypeScript, found by the port and fixed in both.** `deploy run
--redeploy-only` returned `{ kind: 'redeployed', response: ports.redeploy(...) }`
without awaiting a function that returns a promise, so the reply it printed was
the promise itself: the old bundle prints `{}` and exits 0 whatever the server
answered (confirmed against the committed bundle), and a failed request surfaced
as an unhandled rejection instead of `wf: <message>`. It now awaits, prints the
reply, and fails normally. Anything that parsed `{}` from that command (nothing
in the repo does) would see the real reply. Also, for testability only, the TypeScript honours
`WF_DEPLOY_POLL_MS` and `WF_DEPLOY_RETRY_MS` (defaults unchanged: 10 s and 15 s),
which the Rust version reads too.

Not verified: a real VM build. The golden scripts prove the two generators agree,
not that the script itself works on the VM, which is unchanged TypeScript logic.

## 2026-10-06: `wf vm recover` in Rust (tenth port)

`wf vm recover` health-sweeps every app through vm-ops-api, redeploys unhealthy
ones with `recreate=all`, and, when vm-ops-api itself stays unreachable, issues
an OCI soft reboot, then a hard reset, then re-verifies. Native in the Rust `wf`
(`vm_recover.rs`): the five-step plan for `--dry-run`, the reachability window,
the reboot escalation (the OCI calls shell out to the real `oci` CLI exactly as
the TypeScript does: `compute instance action` then `get` until RUNNING, 30
attempts), the sweep (an app is unhealthy when it has no containers, a container
is not running or reports `(unhealthy)`, or its status call fails), the redeploys
and the re-verify window. `--json` and help go to the TypeScript.

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `vm-recover.integration.test.ts`, 16
Given/When/Then tests. Two Node-script stubs stand in for the world: `curl` plays
vm-ops-api (routes by method and path, a queue of replies per route, replies that
only appear once a flag file exists) and `oci` plays the Oracle CLI and drops a
flag file per action, which is what makes vm-ops-api "come back". Covered: the
plan with and without an instance id (flag, `$OCI_INSTANCE_ID`), invalid
`--max-wait`, a healthy sweep (exactly one probe and seven status calls), three
kinds of unhealthy app, an app that never recovers, a failing redeploy, no instance
id, soft reboot sufficient, escalation to a hard reset, nothing helps, an instance
that never reports RUNNING, and an OCI failure. Written first against the
TypeScript bundle, which corrected one of my own expectations (after a reboot the
sweep runs twice: 14 status calls, not 7), then the Rust binary passed all 16.

For testability only the TypeScript honours `WF_RECOVER_POLL_MS`,
`WF_RECOVER_GRACE_MS` and `WF_OCI_POLL_MS` (defaults unchanged: 30 s, 180 s,
5 s); the Rust version reads them too.

Not verified: a real tenancy or a real reboot. This is the one command that can
restart the production VM, so the black-box suite is the only evidence; it has
never been run against OCI.

## Port: `wf deploy agent-deps add|remove|list|replay` to Rust (2026-10-06)

Decision: port the manifest commands natively; `agent-deps install` (an exec job
through vm-ops-api) stays with the TypeScript until `vm exec` helpers are shared.

Benchmark (release build, n=5, `agent-deps list`, one x86_64 4-core container):

| | wall (median) | peak RSS (median) |
|---|---|---|
| TypeScript bundle | 276.2 ms | 64.6 MB |
| Rust | 6.3 ms | 11.0 MB |

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `agent-deps.integration.test.ts`, 12
Given/When/Then tests, green against the TypeScript bundle and the Rust binary.
One generic Node-script stub plays `dpkg-query`, `apt-get`, `npm`, `mise` and
`mkdir`, logs every call and answers from a table. Covered: empty and corrupt
manifests, add (ISO timestamp, pretty file, idempotent), remove, unknown manager,
replay for present, missing and failing entries, the no-stderr failure detail,
`--managers` filtering and its validation.

Known deviation: the Rust replay has no 5 minute install timeout (std `Command`
has none); a hung `apt-get` blocks boot replay until the container supervisor
kills it. Not verified: a real agent-shell container.

## Port: `wf deploy sync-creds` and `wf deploy tf apply|exec` to Rust (2026-10-06)

Decision: port both. `sync-creds` writes the OCI PEM, the managed `terraform.tfvars`
block and `~/.oci/config`; `tf exec` injects the state-bucket credentials (always from
the bitwarden backend) into terraform; `tf apply` keeps the free-tier guard, the
pre-apply state snapshot and the rollback. `agent-deps install` is still TypeScript.

Benchmark (release build, n=5, `tf exec /tmp state list` with stub `bws` and
`terraform`, so the figure is wrapper cost only):

| | wall (median) | peak RSS (median) |
|---|---|---|
| TypeScript bundle | 284.5 ms | 64.7 MB |
| Rust | 20.5 ms | 10.9 MB |

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `infra.integration.test.ts`, 15
Given/When/Then tests, green against the TypeScript bundle and the Rust binary
(whole `tests/integration` directory: 272/272 on both). Node-script stubs play `bws`,
`terraform` (logs argv, cwd and the injected AWS_* env, answers from a per-subcommand
queue) and `oci`; HOME and the repo are temp dirs. Covered: file contents and modes,
managed block replace and append, optional passphrase and PagerDuty keys, missing
secrets, env injection, exit-status passthrough, exit 2 without arguments, an absent
state secret, a clean apply, the guard (pool overflow, terminated and non-A1
instances ignored, billable shape, E2 micro spec), apply failure, rollback with the
pushed state, and a failing plan.

Known deviation: the Rust version has no per-call timeouts (the TypeScript allows
5 min for plan, 10 for apply, 1 for state calls). Not verified: a real tenancy, a
real state bucket or a real `terraform`; `tf apply` has only ever run against stubs.

## Port: `wf tailscale devices|auth-key-status|auth-key rotate (dry run)` to Rust (2026-10-06)

Decision: port the API-backed reads, the device delete and the rotate dry run. `tailscale
up` (needs the local tailscaled) and `auth-key rotate --apply` (a vault write) stay with
the TypeScript. The Rust client uses `curl` for the Tailscale API instead of an HTTP
crate, which keeps the binary at 2.4 MB.

Benchmark (release build, n=5, `auth-key rotate` dry run, credentials from env):

| | wall (median) | peak RSS (median) |
|---|---|---|
| TypeScript bundle | 251.3 ms | 64.5 MB |
| Rust | 7.3 ms | 11.0 MB |

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `tailscale.integration.test.ts`, 21
Given/When/Then tests, green against the TypeScript bundle and the Rust binary. The
Tailscale admin API is a mock server run as a separate Node process (wf is spawned
synchronously, so an in-process server could not answer); both clients honour a new
`WF_TAILSCALE_API` base URL, default unchanged. Covered: verdict lines and the suffix
warning, form-encoded OAuth exchange and bearer use, `--stale-minutes`, an unparseable
`lastSeen`, a missing tailnet id, a refused exchange, the 403 hint, retire as dry run,
`--apply`, `--force`, variadic `--name`, unknown names, key status for healthy, due,
newest-of-two and ownerless keys, `--warn-days`, and the rotate dry run's validation.
The TypeScript oracle corrected one of my expectations (`list` also prints `<-- RETIRE`).

Known deviation: timestamps are parsed as RFC 3339 only; the TypeScript `Date` accepts
more shapes. Not verified: the real Tailscale API.

and `remote-login` watch credential files and the descriptor hooks on this machine, and
stay with the TypeScript. The listener client is `curl`, as for vm-ops-api.

Benchmark (release build, n=5, `auth done s1` against a refused local port, so it covers
vault lookup, request and error path):

| | wall (median) | peak RSS (median) |
|---|---|---|
| TypeScript bundle | 295.5 ms | 69.1 MB |
| Rust | 16.3 ms | 11.0 MB |

Given/When/Then tests, green against the TypeScript bundle and the Rust binary (whole
`tests/integration` directory: 310/310 on both). The listener is a mock HTTP service in
a separate process, now a shared helper (`tests/helpers/mock-http-server.ts`) that the
tailscale suite also uses. Covered: the tool list, the worked-example error for zero or
two spec options, inline and file specs with the trigger headers, trailing slashes,

## Landing on dev: `wf bench` renamed to `wf cli-bench` (2026-10-06)

While rebasing the stack, origin/dev had gained `clis/app-bench`, mounted as `wf bench`
through CLI_REGISTRY. The native Rust `bench` (cold start, wall time, peak RSS of a CLI)
would have shadowed it, so `wf bench build|memory|runtime|compare|list` would have run the
wrong tool. Decision: the CLI benchmark is now `wf cli-bench`, in the TypeScript and in the
Rust binary; `wf bench` is app-bench's again. Checked: `wf bench list` through the Rust
binary reaches app-bench. The black-box suite is 310/310 on both binaries after the rename.

## rca phase 0: import-time memory per dependency (2026-10-06)

What it answers: which of `remote-chat-agent`'s runtime dependencies cost real memory, so the
port plan (`docs/plans/rust-services/remote-chat-agent.md`) knows where the Node resident set
comes from. Method: `node -e "import('<dep>')"` run from `backend/remote-chat-agent`, peak RSS
via `RUSAGE_CHILDREN`, n=3 (n=5 for the empty process), one x86_64 4-core container, node 22.
Each row is that package alone; they are not additive (they share Node internals).

| import | peak RSS (median) | over an empty node |
|---|---|---|
| empty node process | 41.9 MB | 0 |
| playwright | 137.8 MB | +95.9 MB |
| effect | 88.2 MB | +46.3 MB |
| sharp | 68.1 MB | +26.2 MB |
| ws | 63.0 MB | +21.1 MB |
| express | 61.1 MB | +19.2 MB |
| @opencode-ai/sdk | 61.0 MB | +19.1 MB |
| mammoth | 60.2 MB | +18.3 MB |
| whisper-node | 55.8 MB | +13.9 MB |
| shiki | 55.1 MB | +13.2 MB |
| zod | 54.4 MB | +12.5 MB |
| tesseract.js | 52.2 MB | +10.3 MB |
| diff2html | 51.3 MB | +9.4 MB |
| busboy | 50.5 MB | +8.6 MB |
| ffmpeg-static | 43.4 MB | +1.5 MB |
| pdf-parse | 43.0 MB | +1.1 MB |

`pdfjs-dist` is not a valid row: importing it fails in plain Node (`DOMMatrix is not defined`).
The very low `pdf-parse` and `ffmpeg-static` figures are lazy loaders, so they cost their
memory when used, not when imported.

Reading: `playwright` and `effect` dominate, then `sharp`. The HTTP and WebSocket layer
(express, ws, zod) is about 20 MB each on top of the 42 MB runtime floor. That supports the
plan's split: the orchestration engine (built on `effect`) and the HTTP/WS layer are what a
Rust service replaces; the media dependencies are what the on-demand worker keeps out of the
resident set. Not measured: the running service's idle RSS and RSS under load (needs its
environment); that is the next step of phase 0.

## aarch64 check for the Rust wf (2026-10-06)

Part of the distribution gate (the VM is an aarch64 A1 instance). `cargo check --release -p wf
--target aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` finishes cleanly on this x86_64 container (rustc 1.97.0,
`rustup target add` for the std component), covering `wf` and `agent-config-sync` with every
dependency (`ed25519-dalek`, `hmac`, `sha2`, `base64`, `regex`, `serde_json`, `toml`). All are
pure Rust, so no C toolchain is pulled into the build.

Not verified: linking and running an aarch64 binary (no cross linker or aarch64 machine here),
and the `gh-runner` image, which cannot be edited or tested from this session. So the gate is
narrower than before but not closed: the remaining step is `cargo build --release -p wf` on the
VM itself (agent-shell has a persistent Tier-1 cargo prefix at `/workspace/tools/cargo`) and
one black-box run there.

## rca-core: the ladder and the pool ledger in Rust (2026-10-06)

Decision: first slice of the rca plan (`docs/plans/rust-services/remote-chat-agent.md`), the pure
core `wf` and the service both need: failure classification, rung resolution and escalation, and
the pool cooldown ledger (`plugins/ladder/ladder.ts`, `plugins/cooldowns/poolLedger.ts`,
`nextLedger`). New crate `clis/rca-core`, a workspace member, no I/O. It reads the same
`model-ladders.json` the TypeScript imports (one source file, embedded at build time).

Oracle: `clis/rca-core/tests/golden/rca-core.json`, written by the TypeScript implementation
(`backend/remote-chat-agent/tests/unit/plugins/rcaCoreGolden.test.ts`, `UPDATE_GOLDEN=1`). 240
classification cases, 16 escalation, 420 ladder resolutions (every shape against cold pools, pins and
stats), 45 + 13 rung walks over a custom ladder, 5 ledger scripts (cap growth, timeout pairs, the
daily window, recovery) and 630 `nextLedger` cases. The TypeScript test fails when the TypeScript
drifts from the file; `cargo test -p rca-core` fails when the Rust does. Both pass. A deliberate
mutation (the stats threshold 0.8 to 0.9) is caught by the Rust test.

Tier: golden-file comparison across two implementations (unit/contract level). No deployed service
or process boundary is involved yet, so no black-box or runbook tier applies to this slice.

Benchmark: none for this slice, on purpose. It is a library with no entry point; its cost is
measured where it is consumed (`wf dispatch --stats`, `work`, once those are repointed to it).

Known differences: pools are kept sorted in the written ledger where the TypeScript keeps insertion
order (cosmetic, the file stays interchangeable); entries with unknown extra fields lose them on a
round trip; only ISO 8601 timestamps are parsed (the TypeScript `Date` accepts more shapes). Not
done: the file stores and the stats ledger (`ledger.ts`, `stats.ts`), the run plugins, and the
opencode runner.

### rca-core, second slice: run log, stats and the file stores (2026-10-06)

Added to `clis/rca-core`: the run-log row (`runLog.ts`: torn lines and rows without `ts`/`ok`
skipped, legacy rows defaulted), the statistics and their two renderings (`stats.ts`: the
`wf dispatch --stats` table and its `--json` payload, byte for byte), `parseSince`, `filterRuns`,
`shapeStats`, `notionalCost`, and the two file stores (pool ledger with tmp-and-rename, run log
append). The golden file gained a `runLog` section (13 parsed rows, priced and unpriced stats, the
formatted table, the JSON text, 4 shapes, 5 filters, 10 `--since` specs, 20 percentiles, 5 pricing
cases); the TypeScript test regenerates it and the first slice's sections came out unchanged.

The golden file caught one real mistake in my port: a legacy row has no `reason` or `escalated`
in the TypeScript (the defaults omit them), and I had defaulted them. Two JavaScript behaviours
needed exact emulation, both covered by tests: `toFixed(3)` rounds on the exact decimal expansion
with ties up (Rust's formatter rounds ties to even), and `Math.round` rounds halves toward
positive infinity.

Not portable, left out on purpose: `new Date('12')` parses as 2001-12-01 in V8 (a legacy quirk), so
`parseSince` accepts `Nd`/`Nh`/`Nm`, ISO dates and ISO timestamps only; a timestamp without a zone
is read as UTC. Rows whose fields have the wrong type are skipped here where the TypeScript passes
them through. Tier: golden-file comparison plus Rust unit tests for the file stores. No benchmark:
still a library; it is measured through `wf dispatch --stats` once that is repointed.

### First consumer of rca-core: `wf dispatch --stats` (2026-10-06)

`wf dispatch --stats [--since] [--session] [--caller] [--log] [--json]` is native in the Rust `wf`,
built on `rca-core`. Everything else `dispatch` does (a prompt, `--savings`, `--batch`, `--runbook`,
`--clear-cooldown`, `--pricing-sync`) stays with the TypeScript, and so does `--stats` in a sandbox
without `--log` (`CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE=true`, `WF_SITE` not `vm`): there the TypeScript fetches the run
log from the VM ledger first, which the Rust does not do. A `--since` that only JavaScript's `Date`
reads is handed to the TypeScript too.

Benchmark (release build, n=5, a 5,000-row, 3.9 MB run log, `--stats --log <file>`):

| | wall (median) | peak RSS (median) |
|---|---|---|
| TypeScript bundle | 419.7 ms | 95.9 MB |
| Rust | 71.1 ms | 16.0 MB |

The two outputs are byte-identical on that log (table and path line). The Rust figure is dominated by
JSON parsing of the rows; a streaming reader would cut its RSS further and was not needed here.

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `dispatch-stats.integration.test.ts`, 10 Given/When/Then
tests, green on the TypeScript bundle first and then on the Rust binary (whole `tests/integration`
directory: 320/320 on both). Covered: the table with a torn line, a missing log, `--json` (key order,
`15000` not `15000.0`, empty groups as `[]`), ISO and relative `--since`, `--session`/`--caller`
filters and their combination, the unparseable-`--since` failure, `OPENCODE_DISPATCH_RUN_LOG`, and the
default log found from a subdirectory of a checkout. `rca-core` gained `js_json_pretty` and a
`statsFullJson` golden case for the JSON form.

Not verified: the sandbox VM-ledger route, a real run log from the VM.

### `wf dispatch --clear-cooldown <pool>` in Rust (2026-10-06)

Native next to `--stats`. The ledger file is edited as JSON (not through the typed ledger), so other pools,
their key order and any field this version does not know are untouched, as the TypeScript's spread does.
Benchmark (release, n=5, one pool in a one-entry ledger): TypeScript 253.8 ms / 65.4 MB, Rust 6.7 ms / 11.1 MB.
Verification (tier: black-box integration): `dispatch-cooldown.integration.test.ts`, 7 tests, green on the
TypeScript bundle and the Rust binary: cold and warm pools, a pool with no entry, a missing and a corrupt file,
no tmp file left behind, and the default path from a subdirectory of a checkout.

### `wf dispatch --savings` in Rust, and a float-parsing finding (2026-10-06)

`wf dispatch --savings [--since] [--session] [--caller] [--log] [--report] [--ratios] [--json]` is native, with
`computeSavings`, the delegation-report reader and the table in `rca-core` (a `savings` golden section: 4 ratio
tables including a negative-saving one and a borrowed size, with and without a report, plus the empty case).

Benchmark (release build, n=5, the same 5,000-row log, `--report` absent): TypeScript 340.3 ms / 81.9 MB, Rust
53.3 ms / 13.1 MB. `--savings --json` output is byte-identical on that log. Black-box:
`dispatch-savings.integration.test.ts`, 10 tests, green on both binaries; `tests/integration` is 337/337 on both.
One run of the Rust pass showed a single failure in `worker.integration.test.ts` (real git worktrees, 2.2 s into a
parallel run); it passed alone and in two further full runs, so I record it as a load flake, not a regression.

**Finding 1, `serde_json` float parsing.** Without its `float_roundtrip` feature `serde_json` can parse a decimal one
ULP off, which showed up as `savedPct` -109.5 against -109.50000000000001. It would silently skew any cost sum read
from a run log. `rca-core` now enables the feature (features unify across the workspace, so `wf` gets it too). The
golden file is what caught it.

**Finding 2, a bug in the TypeScript, kept for parity.** `runSavings` hands `windowRows` the whole option bag, whose
`caller` defaults to `dispatch`, so without `--caller` only `dispatch` rows are priced even though the savings code
treats `mcp`, `cheap` and `batch` runs as delegations too (the fixture shows 1 of 4 rows counted). The Rust version
reproduces it and the black-box suite documents it; fixing it is a separate, behaviour-changing decision.

JavaScript's `toFixed` keeps the sign of a negative that rounds to zero (`-0.000`); the Rust emulation does too.

### `wf verify --budget` in Rust (2026-10-06)

The simplicity budget (pre-commit jobs, CLAUDE.md and known-gaps lines, dangling CONCEPTS.md links, root scripts,
agent hooks) is native, with `--strict`. `verify --staged`, tier 3 (`verify <sha>`) and `--json` stay with the
TypeScript, as does any budget whose input the Rust cannot read (the TypeScript then words the error). The one new
dependency is `yaml-rust2` for `lefthook.yml` (pure Rust, two small crates); the binary grew from about 2.50 MB to
2.63 MB.

Benchmark (release build, n=5, this repository, `verify --budget`): TypeScript 288.2 ms / 65.5 MB, Rust 14.3 ms /
11.0 MB; the output on the repo is the same six lines and values.

Verification (tier: black-box integration): `verify-budget.integration.test.ts`, 10 tests on a throwaway git repo,
green on the TypeScript bundle and the Rust binary. Covered: all six budgets, the JavaScript line count (`split('\n')`
counts a trailing newline), warn lines on stderr and info lines on stdout, gated against ungated and `--strict`, the
joined failure message, distinct hook commands across events, dangling-link rules (schemes, anchors, lexical `..`,
spaces, absolute paths, empty targets), absent files counting as zero, a missing `pre-commit` section, and a missing
`package.json`.

### rca-core, third slice: the opencode stream and the dispatch summary (2026-10-06)

First pure pieces of the delegation runner (`wf dispatch <prompt>`), the path every delegated task takes:
`parseOpencodeJsonStream` (NDJSON events, hook lines kept as text, the empty-session-id quirk), the one-line tool
summaries (`[shell] ...`, clipped at 140 UTF-16 units), `hasCompletedWrite`, `lastTextPart`, the cap and
transient-crash detectors, the `opencode run` argv (prompt last, which the mock binary relies on), the
`OPENCODE_API_KEY` remap and `formatDispatchSummary` (full and brief). Golden section `opencode`: 5 streams
(one with 25 lines of every event kind, malformed JSON, arrays, unknown types and three kinds of `error` event),
18 detector strings, 16 argv combinations, 8 summary renderings. `toFixed` is now general (`to_fixed(x, digits)`):
the `cost=$` field uses four digits and rounds as JavaScript does.

What is left of the runner, in order: the process attempt (`spawnAttempt.ts`: streaming spawn, an idle watchdog that
resets on output, an absolute ceiling, SIGKILL), the one-shot plugins (output checks, ledger append, cooldown
persistence) and `runWithPlugins`, the remote cooldown mirror and the opencode-go preflight probe, the Claude rung,
then `dispatch.ts` itself and the `wf dispatch <prompt>` entry. The oracle for those is the mock opencode binary
(`libraries/hook-runtime/mock-opencode-bin`) driven through the black-box suite, not a golden file.

### rca-core, fourth slice: the run loop and the one-shot plugin chain (2026-10-06)

`rca-core::run::run_once` fuses `runWithPlugins` with the four one-shot plugins (ledger, cooldowns, ladder, output
checks) and the turn-error plugin, firing their hooks in the TypeScript's order: ladder resolved once per run against a
fresh ledger read, the cooldown snapshot and skip, the attempt, the combined verdict (turn error first, then wrote-nothing,
then the caller's gate), the run-log row written on the first ok or fatal attempt and otherwise by `finishRun`, and the
cooldown ledger learning (`nextLedger`) persisted only when it changed. The caller supplies a `RunHost`: the clock, the two
stores, the diff stat, the output gate and the process attempt, so the same loop serves `wf dispatch <prompt>` and, later,
the service. The run-log row is built in the TypeScript's key order, so the JSONL stays interchangeable.

Oracle: 19 scripted scenarios the TypeScript runs through the real `createOneShotPlugins` and `runWithPlugins` with a fake
clock and in-memory stores (ok on the first rung, cap then ok, two caps escalating, wrote-nothing with and without a diff,
fatal, every rung failing, a cold pool filtered out or ignored when cooldowns are not honored, the bad-output gate, two
instant crashes and two timeouts cooling a pool, an empty ladder, model-missing, a judge call without writes, unstructured
output, a long unicode prompt and padded text). The Rust replay matches every attempt record, every run-log row, every
ledger write and the final ledger. Two deliberate mutations (the summaryChars fallback, the prompt-head length) are caught,
and the first one only after I added the scenarios that exercise them: the first version of the suite did not.

Still to come before `wf dispatch <prompt>` runs natively: the process attempt (streaming spawn, idle watchdog, SIGKILL),
the remote cooldown mirror and the opencode-go preflight (both over the signed vm-ops client the Rust `wf` already has),
the Claude rung (a fallback to the TypeScript when a pool is `claude-max`), the catalog check, and the command itself.
`turnErrorPlugin` is now exported from `dispatch.ts` so the golden test runs the real one.

### Native `wf dispatch <prompt>` (2026-10-06)

`wf dispatch <prompt>` now runs in Rust (`clis/wf/src/dispatch_run.rs`) on top of `rca-core::run::run_once`: the opencode
process attempt with idle, absolute and heartbeat timers, the opencode-go preflight probe, the remote cooldown mirror over
the signed vm-ops client, the transcript, summary and run-log row. Anything it does not own (`--live`, unknown flags, a
runnable `claude-max` rung) is handed to the TypeScript bundle unchanged.

Black-box suite `dispatch-run.integration.test.ts` (22 tests, a stub `opencode` and a mock preflight server) was run on the
TypeScript bundle first; four of my expectations were wrong and were corrected from its real output (the `pool` key in the
run-log row, the quoted `msg="served"`, `--start-model` running to the end of the rotation without wrapping, and the cold
pool not skipping a later rung). Rust then passed unchanged: 22/22, and the whole `tests/integration` directory 369/369 on
both binaries. Tier: integration with stubbed processes; nothing was run against the real opencode or the live pools.

Round trip with a stub opencode, one run each: TypeScript 367 ms / 66 MB, Rust 11 ms / 10 MB. The real cost of a dispatch is
the model call, so this is the wrapper overhead only.

### Native `wf dispatch --pricing-sync` (2026-10-06)

`clis/wf/src/pricing_sync.rs` fetches the OpenRouter model list through `curl`, resolves each ladder model through the same
mapping table, converts per-token rates to per-MTok with the TypeScript's `toFixed(9)` rounding, and writes
`model-pricing.json` atomically with `JSON.stringify(_, null, 2)` formatting. The TypeScript got one seam,
`WF_OPENROUTER_MODELS_URL` (default unchanged), so the black-box suite can point both implementations at a mock server.
`dispatch-pricing-sync.integration.test.ts` (6 tests: conversion and ladder order, exact bytes, unknown models, non-numeric
rates, missing `data`, HTTP error leaves the file untouched) passed on the TypeScript bundle first, then on Rust unchanged.
Tier: integration with a mock HTTP server; the live OpenRouter API was not called. Not benchmarked: one network call
dominates the wall time, and the saving is the same Node start-up already measured for the other commands.

### Native `wf dispatch --batch` (2026-10-06)

`clis/wf/src/dispatch_batch.rs` expands the globs, renders the `{{file}}`/`{{content}}` template and runs the files on a
bounded thread pool (default 2), each through the same `dispatch_one` the single-prompt runner uses (caller `batch`,
transcript per file, no heartbeat), then prints the same table, progress lines and `git diff --stat` tail. The glob matcher
covers `*`, `?`, `**`, literal paths and dot-file rules; patterns with braces, brackets, parentheses, `!`, backslashes,
`..`, or a leading `/` or `./` are handed to the TypeScript's own glob. `wroteNothing` is always false in the TypeScript
result, so the `WROTE0` status can never print there and is not ported.

`dispatch-batch.integration.test.ts` (7 tests: order and table, template without placeholders, recursive pattern with dot
directories, failing file, no match, missing template, concurrency 2 versus 1) passed on the TypeScript bundle first (two
spacing expectations of mine corrected), then on Rust unchanged. Whole `tests/integration` 382/382 on both. Tier:
integration with a stub opencode; no real model call.

Six files with an instant stub opencode, one run each: TypeScript 592 ms / 75 MB, Rust 83 ms / 10 MB (wrapper overhead
only).

### Native `wf verify --staged` (2026-10-06)

The pre-commit gate now runs in Rust (`clis/wf/src/verify.rs`): ESLint per owning package (`pnpm exec eslint` in the package
directory), the simplicity budget (a gated overrun fails the gate before anything else runs, as the TypeScript does by
throwing), the editor-bundle check, then the wf-bundle rebuild and the affected typecheck and `test:unit|flow` through
`pnpm`, with the same affected-package and closure arithmetic over `pnpm ls -r`. The budget inputs are measured before
ESLint runs, so a budget the Rust cannot read hands the whole command to the TypeScript before anything has been linted.
`--files` is supported; anything else falls back.

`verify-staged.integration.test.ts` (11 tests: leaf package with dependents and the wf bundle rebuild, git-index selection
with deletions and non-code excluded, a package nothing depends on, an unowned file, nothing staged, lint failure with the
gate still running, typecheck failure skipping the tests, bundle rebuild failure, budget overrun, cm6 check pass and fail)
passed on the TypeScript bundle first, then on Rust unchanged. Whole `tests/integration` 393/393 on both. Tier:
integration with a stub `pnpm` and a real throwaway git repository; the real ESLint, typecheck and test runs were not
exercised (they are `pnpm`'s).

Gate overhead with a non-code file (budget only), one run each: TypeScript 259 ms / 65 MB, Rust 8 ms / 10 MB. A real
commit adds the `pnpm` work, which this port does not change; the saving is Node start-up plus the `pnpm ls -r` JSON
handling per commit.

### Native `wf caveat caveat-guard` (the Stop hook) (2026-10-06)

`clis/wf/src/caveat_guard/` ports the turn-end judge that runs on every turn: the transcript walk (last assistant text across
split records, last real user request, pending `AskUserQuestion`/`ExitPlanMode` and mid-tool-use skips), the settle polling and
cross-run watermark, the disclosure gate, the circuit breaker, the rolling judge history, the 280-line adversarial prompt, the
verdict parser, the run log and the OTLP wide events. The judge call is the Rust `dispatch_one` (auto off, caller `judge`), so
this is the consumer that the caveat-guard seam to `@app/remote-chat-agent` was waiting for: no HTTP/CLI boundary was needed.
An opencode `session.deleted` payload (its transcript is in opencode's SQLite store), `guard-liveness` and `guard-eval` still
go to the TypeScript, with the already-read stdin handed to it through `legacy_piped`.

Oracles: (1) the existing 31-test hook suite (`caveat-guard.integration.test.ts`, the real hook driven over stdin against the
mock `opencode`) now takes `WF_BIN`; 28 pass and 3 are skipped on the TypeScript bundle and the same on the Rust binary,
unchanged. (2) `caveat-guard-golden.test.ts` has the TypeScript compute 100+ pure results (transcript helpers, noise filter,
log and history parsing, evidence excerpts, verdict parsing, `briefReason`, the block text, five full prompts, the
disclosure texts, OTLP payloads) into `clis/wf/tests/golden/caveat-guard.json`; six Rust unit tests replay it. A one-character
mutation (`briefReason`'s 140 limit) is caught. Writing the oracle found a TypeScript crash: a transcript line that is the
JSON literal `null` throws in `isRealUserTurn`; the Rust treats it as a record without a message. Tier: integration against a
mock `opencode` plus golden unit tests; no real model call, and the OTLP send and the vault path were not exercised (no creds).

One hook invocation (judge allows; the mock opencode answers), including the 150 ms transcript settle: TypeScript 333 ms / 64
MB, Rust 183 ms / 11 MB.

### Native `wf caveat guard-liveness` (2026-10-06)

The UserPromptSubmit liveness check is ported next to the Stop hook (`caveat_guard/liveness.rs`): the previous-turn reader
(completed versus interrupted), the not-firing alarm, the judging receipt and the relayed block line. A new black-box suite,
`guard-liveness.integration.test.ts` (9 tests: first prompt, alarm with no run, alarm naming the last run, a run inside the
one-second tolerance, an interrupted turn, a relayed block with the receipt, a judge outage, no transcript path, unreadable
stdin), passed on the TypeScript bundle first (two expectations of mine corrected, one of them recording that the TypeScript
relays `BLOCKED — [opencode] — reason` with the source tag still attached, the bug its own comment says was fixed), then on
Rust unchanged. The golden oracle gained 9 previous-turn, 9 liveness, 10 last-verdict, 10 receipt and 9 status cases; 7 Rust
unit tests now replay it. Tier: integration plus golden unit tests.

One hook invocation on a three-line transcript: TypeScript 149 ms / 57 MB, Rust 25 ms / 11 MB (this hook runs on every prompt).

### Native `wf secrets set|delete|push-notes|create|update|rename` (2026-10-06)

The vault and keychain writers are Rust now (`clis/wf/src/secrets/{vault,writers}.rs`), so `wf secrets` no longer needs the
TypeScript for any subcommand but `registry sync` and help. They share the read path's registry, backends and telemetry.
Writes go through `bws` with an argv array (no shell) and the separate edit credential, planning is dry unless `--apply`, the
previous value is archived to the owner-only rotation history (`0700` directory, `0600` file) before an update or `set`, and
the 403/404 and EU-host hints are the TypeScript's. The inventory the read path already unions across scopes now also returns
ids and projects, which `create` and `rename` need. One deliberate difference: when `bws` fails with an empty stderr the
TypeScript's message is the command line, which here includes the secret value; the Rust says the exit code instead.

`secrets-writers.integration.test.ts` (32 tests: push-notes dry run, apply, verbose, missing tokens and a refused write;
create with every value source and every refusal; update preview, apply with history modes, missing key and refused write;
rename in place, additive and refusals; set creating and updating; delete on the read-only backend; the macOS backend off
darwin) passed on the TypeScript bundle first (a mask-width miscount and two missing admin-scope fixtures of mine
corrected), then on Rust unchanged. Whole `tests/integration` 426/426 on both. Tier: integration with a stub `bws` and a
temp XDG_STATE_HOME; the real vault and the macOS Keychain were not touched.

`wf secrets push-notes` with an empty vault, one run each: TypeScript 278 ms / 73 MB, Rust 9 ms / 10 MB.

### Native `wf work <slug> --check`, `wf status <slug>`, `wf offload worker check|status` (2026-10-06)

The read-only half of the git-state worker is Rust (`clis/wf/src/worker_plan.rs`): the `units.jsonl` parser and validator
(unit rules, duplicate ids, unknown dependencies, cycles, dependency order), the lease and failed refs read from `origin`
through `git ls-remote` and `git log -1`, the `Unit:` trailers on the land ref, and the status table. Running a plan
(`wf work <slug>`), `knowledge` and `visual` stay with the TypeScript. `wf` repeats a failing worker's stderr as its own
`wf: ...` message, which the Rust reproduces, so a bad plan prints its report twice exactly as before.

`work-plan.integration.test.ts` (9 tests, real git with a bare `origin`: valid plan in dependency order, the worker spelling,
every unit error with its line number, unknown dependency and cycle, empty and missing plans, a bad slug, a status table with
done, fresh, stale, unreadable, failed, blocked and runnable units, an empty remote, and `--land-ref`) passed on the
TypeScript bundle first (my miscounted column width and the duplicated stderr corrected), then on Rust unchanged. Whole
`tests/integration` 435/435 on both. One known difference: a line that is not JSON reports the parser's own message, so the
Rust says `not JSON: <serde message>` where V8 says `Expected property name or '}'...`; the suite asserts the prefix.
Tier: integration with real git against a local bare remote.

`wf work p9 --check`: TypeScript 244 ms / 64 MB, Rust 12 ms / 10 MB.

### App benchmark suite and the refactor it drove (2026-10-06)

Decision: `wf bench` (build, memory, runtime) is the permanent benchmark for the app, with committed `initial` baselines and ratcheted `current` ones; the refactor kept only what moved build speed first, memory second, runtime third.

Evidence (3 runs each, medians; memory and runtime are lab-only, Chromium V8 at iPhone 15 Pro size):
- Build, web export: cold 120.6 to 103.7 s, warm 18.6 to 13.4 s, modules 5,682 to 4,033, JS 9.20 to 7.48 MB raw (1.94 to 1.74 MB gzip). Kept: the lucide barrel was imported by one screen (1,720 modules), now blocked by ESLint in features; the Skia web entry moved behind `lib/skiaWeb`, which also unblocked the iOS production export (11.08 MB Hermes bytecode).
- Memory: cold-start app heap 23.94 to 18.95 MB (-21%) from Metro `inlineRequires`; the 50% target was not reached and the floor is documented. Loop growth is noise (five windows 22.6-23.2 MB). Ruled out without a run: async routes (no effect in the SDK 57 static export, reverted), a Tamil-canvas env default (no effect, reverted).
- Runtime: within noise of `initial` (tap 32.5 vs 32.6 ms, long tasks 11 vs 12); runtime work stopped there by the priority order.
- Cost of the benchmark itself: n=3 per suite, the planted-leak e2e proves the memory suite fails a leak; no model calls.


### `wf --help` and every command's help served by Rust (2026-10-06)

`wf --help`, `wf <command...> --help`, `wf help [command]` and a command group run bare (help on stderr, exit 1) no longer
start Node. The texts are the TypeScript commander tree's own output, walked through `--help` by
`help.integration.test.ts` (112 command paths) into `clis/wf/src/help/*.txt` and a generated `help_table.rs`; the Rust
binary embeds them. The same test fails when the bundle's help drifts from the committed files, so a new or reworded command
cannot silently leave the Rust help stale: regenerate with `UPDATE_GOLDEN=1`. A command whose own `--help` is forwarded to a
sibling CLI (`caveat`, the passthroughs) is not in the table and keeps its own handling. The 4 tests assert stdout and exit
codes byte for byte against the table, with the bundle and with `WF_BIN`; whole `tests/integration` 439/439 on both. Cost:
the binary grew from 2.63 MB to 3.08 MB for the embedded text.

`wf --help`: TypeScript 264 ms / 64 MB, Rust 3 ms / 10 MB.

### Native `wf log emit` (the `logger`) (2026-10-06)

`clis/wf/src/log_emit.rs` sends one OTLP log record to the BetterStack ingest (`curl` with the token on stdin, never argv,
bypassing a sandbox proxy as the TypeScript's re-exec did), scrubs it first (sensitive-named keys, the values of
sensitive-named environment variables of 8+ characters wherever they appear, and e-mail, JWT and card-number shapes), and
echoes the line the way Node's `console.info(message, data)` prints it, including `util.inspect`'s quoting and its
80-column break rule for the attribute object. A `%` in the message, or a long multi-line attribute value (where `util.format`
and `inspect` would do something the port does not reproduce), goes to the TypeScript.

The oracle run found three defects in the TypeScript, all recorded rather than copied:
1. **The scrubber never ran.** `emitLog` handed `configureLogger` a `root()` that built a new logger on every call, so the
   scrubber plugin was attached to a throwaway instance and the line that was sent was never scrubbed: a token or e-mail
   in a message reached BetterStack. Fixed in the TypeScript (one root per call) so both implementations scrub; the suite
   proves it.
2. **`{"sent":true}` for a rejected export.** Against a server answering 401, 400 or 500 it still printed `sent:true`. The
   Rust says `{"sent":false,"reason":"export failed: HTTP 401"}`; this is the one Rust-only test.
3. **30 seconds per call.** The TypeScript process stays alive about 30 s after a successful export, even against a local
   server (measured), so its suite takes 4.5 minutes for 9 tests and runs by default only with `WF_BIN`
   (`WF_SLOW_TESTS=1` for the bundle: 9/9 there too). The Rust run is 0.8 s for the same 9.

Tier: integration against a local HTTPS server with a throwaway `openssl` certificate; the real BetterStack was not called.
Whole `tests/integration`: 449/449 on Rust, 439 passed and 10 skipped (the slow suite) on the bundle.

`wf log emit` against the local server: TypeScript 30,200 ms, Rust 36 ms / 12 MB (the TypeScript peak RSS was not recorded for this run).

## 2026-10-06: `wf browser` thin client in Rust

Decision: port the multi-browser CLI as a Rust HTTP client of the existing Node/Playwright daemon (the daemon stays a sidecar, which the brief allows). Every command becomes one `POST /call`; a missing or dead daemon is restarted under `timeout` with the same registry and slot rules. Parse irregularities, `--schema`, `goldens`, `preflight`, `tunnel`, `start --foreground`, `navigate --dev-bridge` and unknown devices still forward to the TS bundle.

Oracle: `browser-client.integration.test.ts` (53 tests) against a fake daemon (`tests/helpers/mock-daemon.ts`), green on the TS bundle and the Rust binary. Whole integration directory: 502/502 on Rust; the TS run passes 492 with 10 slow log-emit tests skipped.

Benchmark (`browser list-devices`, n=5 median, wrapper only): TS 1371 ms / 166 MB, Rust 9 ms / 12 MB.

Boundary: `clis/multi-browser/harness-shell/ui` is React TSX served to a browser and cannot become Rust, so "no TS under clis/" needs an exemption or a move out of `clis/`.

Oracle: `metro-teardown.integration.test.ts` (17 tests, stub `lsof`/`curl`/`kill`, throwaway registry) plus the existing `metro.integration.test.ts`; 23/23 on the TS bundle and on the Rust binary. Differences found by running the TS first: `clear-cache` reports the `metro-cache` path in its closing line.

Benchmark (`metro status`, n=5 median, wrapper only): TS 257 ms / 65 MB, Rust 13 ms / 12 MB.

Decision: port the local docs/gaps and docs/solutions ledger (frontmatter via yaml-rust2). `index --gemini` and `which` call Google Docs and stay in the TypeScript. The TS host re-prints a failing worker's stderr as `wf: <tail>`, which the Rust copies.

Follow-up, same day: `wf knowledge which <path> [--manifest p]` also runs in Rust for the hit path; a missing manifest or an uncovered path forwards to the TS, which words the error. 4 more black-box tests (20/20 on both binaries); output identical to the TS on this repo's real manifest. `index --gemini` still needs gcloud and Google Docs and stays in the TS.

## 2026-10-06: the Rust binary becomes `wf`

Decision (user-approved): `.agents/setup.sh` now links the Rust binary as `wf` as soon as cargo builds it, keeping `wf-rs` as an alias. Without cargo the TS bundle link from `step_wf` stays, so a machine without a toolchain keeps working. Commands the Rust binary has not ported exec the TS bundle it finds beside the repo (`WF_LEGACY_BUNDLE` overrides). Direct callers of `bundle/wf.js` (Dockerfiles, deploy scripts, CI) are unchanged and keep working until their images build the Rust binary.

Gate before the switch: black-box integration suite 561/561 on the Rust binary (551 plus 10 slow log-emit tests skipped on the TS bundle), `cargo test` green. `wf --version` now answers `0.1.0` like the TS; two stale Rust dispatch tests that used `secrets --help` (now served natively) were moved to `report-health`.
